People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me today. Um, well, I hope I'm explaining this right, but... I guess what brings me in is that I just feel like I'm always moving from one thing to the next. I’ve always been the kind of guy who just likes to stay busy, you know? I can't really just... idle very well. 

And usually, that's a good thing! It means I'm a hard worker. But, um, my job—I do customer service—we recently got a new work setup, and... well, it's just made it a lot harder to hide. I don't want anyone to get the wrong impression of me at work, you know? Does that make sense? I hope I'm in the right place for this.

Differential includes ADHD (F90.9) as primary consideration given lifelong pattern of needing to stay busy; generalized anxiety disorder (F41.1) as restlessness can be anxiety-driven; personality disorder traits (F60.80) given long-standing pattern; impulse control issues (F63.9); and conduct-related concerns (F91.9) though less likely. Well, um, I guess it's a little bit of both, if that's okay to say? I mean, I definitely like to stay active, which I think makes me a good employee, right? But, um, when it comes to the focusing part... I try really hard, I do. 

But sometimes, like... if we have one of those longer coaching meetings, or if they hand me a whole new procedure to read through... I always start off really well! But then, maybe a few minutes in, I'll realize my eyes are just sort of moving over the words, or I'm nodding along, but I didn't actually take in what was just said or read. Was this something you noticed about yourself as a kid in school, or is this more something that's developed over time as an adult?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... well, to be completely honest with you, Doctor—and I really hope this doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker—but it's definitely been around long before this recent job change. \n\nI guess if I really look back, it goes all the way back to grade school. My teachers... well, they were very nice, and they always made a point to write that I was a bright kid, which my parents were always proud of! I think it really is just... well, like you said, how I'm wired naturally. \n\nLike, if I have to sit still through one of those long training videos at work, or if I'm stuck waiting in a really slow appointment... I just start to feel kind of, um, boxed in? I don't know if that's the right way to describe it, but I just get this strong feeling like I really need to stand up and stretch, or at least be doing something with my hands. \n\nIt's not that I'm worrying about anything or dreading the training, I just like to be engaged and have a lot of natural energy, I guess. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this is becoming more noticeable at your new job setup. Besides work, how has this need to stay busy and difficulty focusing affected other areas of your life—like your relationships, hobbies, or getting things done at home?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really try to be a good husband, you know? My wife is wonderful, and we have a great relationship, I promise! But, um, I guess at home... well, when we try to do quiet stuff that’s supposed to be relaxing? Like, if we're just reading in the living room, or sitting through a movie she really wanted to watch... I try my best to just sit and relax with her. \n\nBut it usually doesn’t last too long before I’m, you know, shifting around on the couch, or making little comments about what's happening on the screen, or... getting up to grab a water or check on something in the kitchen. I really don't mean to be disruptive to her, I just... I like to be doing something, I guess. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad partner? I really do enjoy spending time with her! Is it okay to struggle with the quiet, relaxing stuff like that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right examples.\n\n[Last Question]\nYou mentioned trying to manage this at work and at home with your wife. Well, um... I really hate to admit this, because I always want to do a good job. I really pride myself on being a hard worker, and I get along just wonderfully with the customers on the phone—they always tell me how friendly I am! But, I guess... well, to be completely honest, it has started showing up a little bit in my recent performance reviews at work. 

My supervisors—who are very fair, I don't want to complain about them at all—they've had to point out that I have some unfinished tickets at the end of the day. And, um, I guess I've made a few little mistakes here and there that end up slowing the team down. I feel just terrible about it, because I really am trying my absolute hardest, I promise! I don't want to be a burden on my coworkers. 

I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee? I just want to be totally upfront with you so you have all the right information. Is it normal for it to affect work like that?

But then, maybe a few minutes in, I'll realize my eyes are just sort of moving over the words, or I'm nodding along, but I didn't actually take in what was just said or read. It's not that I don't care, I really want to do a good job. It's just... a little tricky sometimes. Is that normal? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm a bad worker.\n\nDoctor: How far back do you remember having this difficulty with focus and the need to stay busy? Was this something you noticed about yourself as a kid in school, or is this more something that's developed over time as an adult?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... well, to be completely honest with you, Doctor—and I really hope this doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker—but it's definitely been around long before this recent job change. \n\nI guess if I really look back, it goes all the way back to grade school. My teachers... well, they were very nice, and they always made a point to write that I was a bright kid, which my parents were always proud of! But, um, they would also write that I could be a little... careless sometimes. And that I talked out of turn, or that they had to keep reminding me to just stay with my work. \n\nI really wasn't trying to be a bad student, I promise! I just had a lot of enthusiasm and wanted to be involved in whatever was going on in the room. I hope that doesn't paint a bad picture of me. Is it normal for it to go back that far? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting still or trying to focus on something, do you feel anxious or worried about things, or does the restlessness and urge to move feel more like it's just how your brain naturally works without the anxiety piece?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm anxious. I'm actually a pretty upbeat guy, I promise! I really hope I don't come across as a nervous person or a worrywart. I think it really is just... well, like you said, how I'm wired naturally. \n\nLike, if I have to sit still through one of those long training videos at work, or if I'm stuck waiting in a really slow appointment... I just start to feel kind of, um, boxed in? I don't know if that's the right way to describe it, but I just get this strong feeling like I really need to stand up and stretch, or at least be doing something with my hands. \n\nIt's not that I'm worrying about anything or dreading the training, I just like to be engaged and have a lot of natural energy, I guess. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this is becoming more noticeable at your new job setup. Besides work, how has this need to stay busy and difficulty focusing affected other areas of your life—like your relationships, hobbies, or getting things done at home?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really try to be a good husband, you know? My wife is wonderful, and we have a great relationship, I promise! But, um, I guess at home... well, when we try to do quiet stuff that’s supposed to be relaxing? Like, if we're just reading in the living room, or sitting through a movie she really wanted to watch... I try my best to just sit and relax with her. \n\nBut it usually doesn’t last too long before I’m, you know, shifting around on the couch, or making little comments about what's happening on the screen, or... getting up to grab a water or check on something in the kitchen. I really don't mean to be disruptive to her, I just... I like to be doing something, I guess. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad partner? I really do enjoy spending time with her! Is it okay to struggle with the quiet, relaxing stuff like that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right examples.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to manage this at work and at home with your wife. Overall, would you say these difficulties with focus and needing to stay busy have caused real problems or gotten in the way of things that are important to you—like affecting your job performance, your relationships, or your ability to complete things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really hate to admit this, because I always want to do a good job. I really pride myself on being a hard worker, and I get along just wonderfully with the customers on the phone—they always tell me how friendly I am! But, I guess... well, to be completely honest, it has started showing up a little bit in my recent performance reviews at work. \n\nMy supervisors—who are very fair, I don't want to complain about them at all—they've had to point out that I have some unfinished tickets at the end of the day. And, um, I guess I've made a few little mistakes here and there that end up slowing the team down. I feel just terrible about it, because I really am trying my absolute hardest, I promise! I don't want to be a burden on my coworkers. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee? I just want to be totally upfront with you so you have all the right information. Is it normal for it to affect work like that?\n\nDoctor: When you're in a meeting or conversation with someone, do you find yourself interrupting people or blurting things out before they finish speaking, or is it mainly just the difficulty concentrating and the restlessness?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... well, I really pride myself on being polite. I was always taught to be a good listener, I promise! But, um... I guess sometimes, maybe I do... jump the gun a little bit? \n\nLike, at the office, if I hear a couple of my coworkers trying to work through a tricky customer problem near my desk, I’ll just... well, I'll kind of jump right in with an answer before they’ve even finished talking. Or, you know, before anyone actually asked me for my input. \n\nI really, really don’t mean to be rude or step on anyone's toes! I just get so eager to help them out, the solution just sort of pops out of my mouth before I can stop it. I always try to apologize right away. I hope they know I'm just trying to be helpful and be a good team player... does that make sense? Is it normal to get a little too eager to help like that?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Oh, um... organizing things? Well, I really try my best to be organized! I actually have a whole system of sticky notes that I use at my desk, and I always make sure to write everything down so I don't forget. I want to make sure I'm doing things the right way, you know?

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, and welcome. I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, Doctor. Thank you so much for taking the time to see me today. Um, well, I hope I'm explaining this right, but... I guess what brings me in is that I just feel like I'm always moving from one thing to the next. I’ve always been the kind of guy who just likes to stay busy, you know? I can't really just... idle very well. \n\nAnd usually, that's a good thing! It means I'm a hard worker. But, um, my job—I do customer service—we recently got a new work setup, and... well, it's just made it a lot harder to hide. I don't want anyone to get the wrong impression of me at work, you know? Does that make sense? I hope I'm in the right place for this.\n\nDoctor: When you're trying to focus on one task at work, do you find it hard to keep your attention on it, or is it more that you feel the urge to move around and do different things even when you're able to concentrate?\n\nPatient: Well, um, I guess it's a little bit of both, if that's okay to say? I mean, I definitely like to stay active, which I think makes me a good employee, right? But, um, when it comes to the focusing part... I try really hard, I do. \n\nBut sometimes, like... if we have one of those longer coaching meetings, or if they hand me a whole new procedure to read through... I always start off really well! But then, maybe a few minutes in, I'll realize my eyes are just sort of moving over the words, or I'm nodding along, but I didn't actually take in what was just said or read. It's not that I don't care, I really want to do a good job. It's just... a little tricky sometimes. Is that normal? I hope that doesn't sound like I'm a bad worker.\n\nDoctor: How far back do you remember having this difficulty with focus and the need to stay busy? Was this something you noticed about yourself as a kid in school, or is this more something that's developed over time as an adult?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... well, to be completely honest with you, Doctor—and I really hope this doesn't make me sound like I was a troublemaker—but it's definitely been around long before this recent job change. \n\nI guess if I really look back, it goes all the way back to grade school. My teachers... well, they were very nice, and they always made a point to write that I was a bright kid, which my parents were always proud of! But, um, they would also write that I could be a little... careless sometimes. And that I talked out of turn, or that they had to keep reminding me to just stay with my work. \n\nI really wasn't trying to be a bad student, I promise! I just had a lot of enthusiasm and wanted to be involved in whatever was going on in the room. I hope that doesn't paint a bad picture of me. Is it normal for it to go back that far? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right information.\n\nDoctor: When you're sitting still or trying to focus on something, do you feel anxious or worried about things, or does the restlessness and urge to move feel more like it's just how your brain naturally works without the anxiety piece?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, I wouldn't say I'm anxious. I'm actually a pretty upbeat guy, I promise! I really hope I don't come across as a nervous person or a worrywart. I think it really is just... well, like you said, how I'm wired naturally. \n\nLike, if I have to sit still through one of those long training videos at work, or if I'm stuck waiting in a really slow appointment... I just start to feel kind of, um, boxed in? I don't know if that's the right way to describe it, but I just get this strong feeling like I really need to stand up and stretch, or at least be doing something with my hands. \n\nIt's not that I'm worrying about anything or dreading the training, I just like to be engaged and have a lot of natural energy, I guess. Does that make sense? I hope I'm answering your question the right way.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned this is becoming more noticeable at your new job setup. Besides work, how has this need to stay busy and difficulty focusing affected other areas of your life—like your relationships, hobbies, or getting things done at home?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really try to be a good husband, you know? My wife is wonderful, and we have a great relationship, I promise! But, um, I guess at home... well, when we try to do quiet stuff that’s supposed to be relaxing? Like, if we're just reading in the living room, or sitting through a movie she really wanted to watch... I try my best to just sit and relax with her. \n\nBut it usually doesn’t last too long before I’m, you know, shifting around on the couch, or making little comments about what's happening on the screen, or... getting up to grab a water or check on something in the kitchen. I really don't mean to be disruptive to her, I just... I like to be doing something, I guess. I hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad partner? I really do enjoy spending time with her! Is it okay to struggle with the quiet, relaxing stuff like that? I just want to make sure I'm giving you the right examples.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned trying to manage this at work and at home with your wife. Overall, would you say these difficulties with focus and needing to stay busy have caused real problems or gotten in the way of things that are important to you—like affecting your job performance, your relationships, or your ability to complete things you need to do?\n\nPatient: Well, um... I really hate to admit this, because I always want to do a good job. I really pride myself on being a hard worker, and I get along just wonderfully with the customers on the phone—they always tell me how friendly I am! But, I guess... well, to be completely honest, it has started showing up a little bit in my recent performance reviews at work. \n\nMy supervisors—who are very fair, I don't want to complain about them at all—they've had to point out that I have some unfinished tickets at the end of the day. And, um, I guess I've made a few little mistakes here and there that end up slowing the team down. I feel just terrible about it, because I really am trying my absolute hardest, I promise! I don't want to be a burden on my coworkers. \n\nI hope that doesn't make me sound like a bad employee? I just want to be totally upfront with you so you have all the right information. Is it normal for it to affect work like that?\n\nDoctor: When you're in a meeting or conversation with someone, do you find yourself interrupting people or blurting things out before they finish speaking, or is it mainly just the difficulty concentrating and the restlessness?\n\nPatient: Oh, gosh... well, I really pride myself on being polite. I was always taught to be a good listener, I promise! But, um... I guess sometimes, maybe I do... jump the gun a little bit? \n\nLike, at the office, if I hear a couple of my coworkers trying to work through a tricky customer problem near my desk, I’ll just... well, I'll kind of jump right in with an answer before they’ve even finished talking. Or, you know, before anyone actually asked me for my input. \n\nI really, really don’t mean to be rude or step on anyone's toes! I just get so eager to help them out, the solution just sort of pops out of my mouth before I can stop it. I always try to apologize right away. I hope they know I'm just trying to be helpful and be a good team player... does that make sense? Is it normal to get a little too eager to help like that?\n\nDoctor: Besides jumping into conversations at work, do you find yourself acting on impulses in other situations too—like making quick decisions you later regret, or doing things without thinking through the consequences? How often does that happen?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... quick decisions? Well, I don't think so. I really try to be careful and think things through, especially when it comes to anything important. I'm not the type to, you know, go out and make big, reckless choices or do anything dangerous. I try to be very responsible! \n\nI guess... well, no, nothing really comes to mind about doing things without thinking of the consequences. I mean, maybe I'll occasionally buy a little treat at the grocery store checkout that wasn't on the list, but... everyone does that, right? That's not what you mean, is it? I just want to make sure I'm understanding you correctly. I really don't think I have a problem with bad impulses or anything like that. I hope that's the right kind of answer?\n\nDoctor: When you have a project or task that requires planning out multiple steps—like organizing your work tickets for the day or planning something at home—do you find it hard to get started or to organize how you're going to do it, or does that part come pretty naturally to you?\n\nPatient: Oh, um... organizing things? Well, I really try my best to be organized! I actually have a whole system of sticky notes that I use at my desk, and I always make sure to write everything down so I don't forget. I want to make sure I'm doing things the right way, you know?\n\nI guess... well, if I'm being perfectly honest, sometimes getting started can take a little bit of time. Like, if I have a big pile of tickets, I might spend a little while just kind of sorting them out, or maybe I'll tidy up my desk first so I have a clean workspace. I just want to make sure everything is perfect before I dive in. But once I get going, I really give it my all! \n\nI don't think it's a huge problem or anything. I mean, everybody likes to get their ducks in a row before they start a big project, don't they? I just want to make sure I'm doing a good job and not letting anyone down. Does that sound like what you were asking about? I hope I'm explaining it okay!\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, well, I really do try to sit still and be respectful! I know it's important to look professional, especially when someone is giving a presentation or talking to the group. I always make an effort to stay in my seat, I promise. 

But, um... I guess if you were to look closely, maybe I'm not always completely still? Like, even when I'm really trying to look calm and collected, I might have a foot bouncing under the desk, or... well, sometimes I catch myself clicking a pen during meetings. I don't even really notice I'm doing it until I hear the click, you know? 

I really try to keep it subtle, though! I don't want to be a distraction to anyone else. It's just a little nervous energy, I think. A lot of people have little habits like that to help them focus, right? I hope it doesn't make me seem unprofessional or anything. I'm always trying my best to be a good employee! Is it okay that I do that just a little bit?

Oh, um... grade school? Wow, that's going back a bit. I... well, I was a good kid! I really always tried to please my teachers and be a good student. I wasn't a troublemaker or anything like that.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Oh, um... yeah, I think we just touched on that a little bit, didn't we? I hope I'm not repeating myself too much! But yes, looking back... it definitely started way before this current job.

But... they would also sometimes write that I could be a little, um, careless with my work.
